The revised Speech Learning Model (SLM-r) proposed by Flege and Bohn in 2021 is an updated version of the original Speech Learning Model (SLM) that aims to explain how second language (L2) learners perceive and produce speech sounds in their non-native language. Here's a summary of the key points of the SLM-r:\n\n1. Perceptual assimilation: Similar to the original SLM, the SLM-r posits that L2 learners initially perceive L2 sounds in terms of their native language (L1) sound categories. This process is called perceptual assimilation. The SLM-r, however, introduces a more nuanced view of perceptual assimilation, taking into account the acoustic properties of L2 sounds and the learners' L1 background.\n- Original SLM: L2 sounds are perceived based on similarity to L1 categories.\n- SLM-r: Adds a more nuanced view, considering the acoustic properties of L2 sounds and the complexity of L1 influences (Flege, 1995; Bohn & Flege, 2021).\n\n\n2. Category formation and separation: The SLM-r suggests that L2 learners form new categories for L2 sounds that are not perceptually assimilated to L1 categories. This process involves creating a new phonetic category that is separate from existing L1 categories. The SLM-r emphasizes the role of attention and working memory in this process.\n- Original SLM: L2 learners form new categories for unfamiliar L2 sounds.\n- SLM-r: Emphasizes cognitive challenges in forming new categories, influenced by attention, memory, and learner differences (Flege & Bohn, 2021).\n\n3. Speech production: The SLM-r proposes that L2 speech production is influenced by both perceptual and motor processes. Learners' L1 speech motor patterns can interfere with the production of L2 sounds, leading to accented speech. The SLM-r highlights the importance of sensorimotor integration in L2 speech production.\n- Original SLM: Production is based on the phonetic categories formed during perception, often leading to an accent.\n- SLM-r: Incorporates motor processes, recognizing interference from L1 speech patterns and emphasizing sensorimotor integration (Bohn & Flege, 2021).\n\n4. Younger learners are more likely to form new phonetic categories and produce L2 sounds more accurately than older learners.
